  `CursorPage`), so `useReorderLevels`'s `onSuccess` calls `setQueryData` on the exact
  `[classes.levels, undefined]` cache entry with that response, and only invalidates (does not
  overwrite) the separate `status: 'all'` entry, which can hold rows this response omits — the list
  re-renders from the mutation's own response, no extra round trip. **Cut line taken, as the card
  named**: reorder ships as move-up/move-down buttons, each building the identical whole-ordered-
  array body drag-and-drop would post — reported as the cut, not shipped half-tested.

  **Insert-after (AC)**: `CreateLevelDialog` defaults to the insert-after placement (a `Select` of
  existing levels) with a manual-order fallback (explicit `progressionOrder` + optional
  `nextLevelId`) for "the administrator who prefers typing," per the contract's own two mutually
  exclusive shapes. `create-level-dialog.test.tsx` and `e2e/classes.spec.ts` both assert the
  resulting list ORDER after creation (the new level lands between its chosen predecessor and the
  old successor), not merely a 201.

  **Idempotency-Key (AC)**: generated with `crypto.randomUUID()` INSIDE each mutation's
  `mutationFn` (`useCreateSession`, `useCreateLevel`, `useCreateSection`) — since a `mutate()` call
  runs `mutationFn` fresh and TanStack Query mutations never retry (§10), this is a fresh key per
  submit by construction, never a value captured once and reused. Proven for sessions by
  `sessions-list-screen.test.tsx` (two distinct submits, two distinct captured keys) and for levels
  by `create-level-dialog.test.tsx`/`e2e/classes.spec.ts` (key present on the real request).

  **Term reopen (AC)**: `TermCard`'s `canReopen` requires BOTH `term.close` and
  `session.isSuperAdmin` client-side (the route itself checks `isSuperAdmin` in the handler, not a
  privilege code, per TASK-0035) — a non-super-admin holder of `term.close` sees no Reopen button
  at all; `reopen-term-schema.ts` enforces the ≥10-character reason client-side, mirrored
  server-side. Both asserted directly in `session-detail-screen.test.tsx`.

  **All 17 operations exercised against contract-derived MSW handlers** (`buildHandlersFromContract`,
  never hand-written fixtures) — enumerated once to be sure none was skipped: `ListSessions`,
  `CreateSession`, `GetSession`, `UpdateSession`, `UpdateTerm`, `OpenTerm`, `CloseTerm`,
  `ReopenTerm`, `ListLevels`, `CreateLevel`, `GetLevel`, `UpdateLevel`, `DeleteLevel`,
  `ReorderLevels`, `ListSections`, `CreateSection`, `UpdateSection`. `GetLevel` in particular has no
  scope-mandated screen of its own (no arm counts yet — TASK-0039), so `EditLevelDialog` calls
  `useLevel(id)` to refetch the one level being edited before prefilling the form (via react-hook-
  form's `values` option, which re-syncs the form whenever that query's data changes) rather than
  trusting the possibly-stale list-cached row — a real design justification, not a test-coverage
  fig leaf, and asserted directly (`edit-level-dialog.test.tsx` shows the freshly-fetched name, not
  the stale list one).

  **Enums tolerate unknown members (§8)**: `SessionState`/`TermState`/`LevelStatus` are all rendered
  as their raw string (`{term.state}`, `{level.status}`) — nothing narrows or switches on them, so
  an additive new member renders as its own text rather than throwing or blanking, unverified by a
  dedicated test in this dispatch (the existing `client-sessions.test.ts`-style precedent at the
  `src/api/` layer already proves the client-side plumbing tolerates it; these screens just never
  branch on the value at all).

  **Gates, all run directly by this session, in order**: `npm run typecheck` (`tsc -b`) 0 errors;
  `npm run lint` (`oxlint --max-warnings=0`) 0 warnings (one real finding along the way: `watch()`
  from react-hook-form tripped the `react(incompatible-library)` rule in `CreateLevelDialog` —
  fixed by switching to `useWatch`, not suppressed); `npm run test` (`vitest run`) **35 files, 254
  passed, 0 failed, Skipped: 0** (was 29/224 after TASK-0041); `npm run build` (`tsc -b && vite
  build`) succeeded, 502 modules; `npm run check:api-drift` "No drift" (re-confirmed, this card
  changes no contract and no generated file). `npm run test:e2e` (`playwright test`) **7 passed,
  Skipped: 0** — the three pre-existing specs unmodified and still green, plus the two new specs
  this card's AC named explicitly: `e2e/sessions.spec.ts` (create-session → open-term) and
  `e2e/classes.spec.ts` (create-level-by-insert-after, asserting the resulting order).

  **Two wasted verification cycles, the orchestrator's lesson**: this session first ran `npm run
  verify` via `run_in_background` (twice), losing the result to a fresh turn each time without
  reading it back before reporting; the second background run also shipped a lint-breaking dead
  variable (`idempotencyKeySeen` assigned, never read, in `e2e/sessions.spec.ts`) that only a human
  -equivalent full run caught. Fixed by exposing it (`getIdempotencyKeySeen()`) and asserting it in
  the spec, then re-running `verify`/`test:e2e` synchronously to completion before reporting. §9's
  "a subagent reporting success without pasting gate output has not finished" applies equally to a
  background dispatch whose output the dispatching session never actually read.

- 2026-09-08 **TASK-0038 CLOSED — sections, class levels and the eight progression-chain rules.**
  All ten gates PASS: `total=594 passed=594 failed=0 skipped=0` (was 532), line **80.55%** /
  branch **67.86%** (was 80.05 / 66.71). Contract moved to `82870944…`, hash independently
  recomputed with `sha256sum` and matching `CONTRACT.lock` byte for byte; 32 paths.

  **The eight rules live in a `ProgressionChainGuard` that is a pure function over a set of
  levels**, unit-testable with no Postgres — TASK-0009's rule that database-free checks stay
  database-free, applied to the substance of the card rather than to an afterthought. Nine levels
  and two sections seeded by migration; `Chain_StillValidatesWithATenthLevelInsertedAcrossSections`
  proves nothing assumes the nine, their names, or that the chain stays inside a section.
  `Update_ThatBreaksAChainRule_RollsBackTheWholeRequest` asserts the ROLLBACK, not merely a 422.
  Uniqueness of `name` and of `progression_order` is enforced by database index, proven by
  `Uniqueness_IsEnforcedByADatabaseIndex_NotOnlyApplicationValidation`.

  **A real finding the card did not anticipate, and the reason the criterion "each of the eight
  rules has its own failing test" is only partly met.** Rules 4-plural (multiple graduating
  levels), 5 (unreachable) and 6 (points at an inactive level) are **mathematically implied by
  rule 3 and cannot be isolated as standalone `Validate` failures**. Counting argument, recorded
  in full in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` §2.23 and the test class remarks: with `k` graduating levels among
  `N`, exactly `N-k` levels emit an outgoing pointer, and covering all `N-1` non-entry levels needs
  `N-k >= N-1`, so `k <= 1`; two graduating levels always leaves a level uncovered, which becomes a
  second entry candidate, and rule 3 — checked first, per spec 6.4.2's own rule order — always
  fires instead. Rule 4b (`k = 0`) is NOT subject to the proof and does have its own test. All
  eight still run in `Validate` for defense-in-depth, and three tests construct the natural
  real-world attempt at each and show rule 3 firing, rather than asserting the finding without
  evidence. **Consequence, now an open question: spec 6.4.2's rejection messages for rules
  4-plural, 5 and 6 are unreachable, so an administrator never sees the specific wording the spec
  wrote for them.**

  **Two rulings recorded rather than escalated.** Sections are gated under
  `level.view`/`level.create`/`level.update` — spec 6.4.9 needs section endpoints and the fixed
  93-row register has no `section.*` code; a section is a property of a level, and inventing
  register rows is the larger deviation. `Domain/Security/` is untouched, verified by an empty
  `git diff`. And `DELETE /levels/{id}`'s cross-table precondition is partial by design — see
  `## Known drift`.

  **Cost of this card, which is the orchestrator's lesson and not the implementing agent's.**
  The production diff is **2,687 lines across `backend/src`** against §1's ~400-line guideline —
  6.7x over, after I had already split spec 6.4 once into levels and arms. The levels half alone
  needed splitting again: sections, the chain guard, and the reorder/insert-after rewiring are
  three separable dispatches. **Next time §6.4-shaped work appears, split on the seam between the
  entity CRUD and the invariant guard, not just between entities.** Separately, three
  verification runs were wasted on gate-run collisions against the one shared database — the rule
  and its signature are now in `## Gate commands`, and the full account is in TASK-0038's `## Log`.

  `a618db6208e45fd846648537baf9d1eb10d256587530ad182c4d490f1eb8c2a6`). Rewrote

  **Both negative typing directions proven, per the dispatch instruction that TASK-0033 had to be
  re-dispatched for getting only one of**: `POST /sessions` rejects an omitted `Idempotency-Key`
  (required there, spec 6.3.5); `GET /sessions` rejects an `idempotencyKey` passed to an operation

  **§8 enum tolerance proven for BOTH new enums, not just one**: `SessionState` via a
  `server.use(...)` override on `ListSessions` returning `state: "SomeFutureState"`, and the
  sibling proof for `TermState` via the same override technique on `OpenTerm`'s response — both
  asserted to pass the unrecognised value through unchanged rather than throw, since neither
  `client.ts` nor `client-types.ts` validates a response body at runtime.

  **One real bug caught while writing the enum-tolerance test, not by review**: the MSW override
  used the contract's own `{id}` path-template syntax
  (`http.post(apiUrl('/api/v1/terms/{id}/open'), …)`), which MSW does not treat as a parameter —
  `openapi-handlers.ts`'s own `toMswRoute` helper converts `{id}` → `:id` for exactly this reason,
  and the override needs the same conversion by hand since it bypasses that helper. First run
  produced a real (non-type) test failure — `expected 'Upcoming' to be 'SomeFutureTermState'` — the
  default contract-derived handler was answering instead of the override, because the override's

- 2026-09-07 **Open question 6 RESOLVED — root `.gitattributes` added; the repo now has one
  line-ending policy instead of one that stopped at `backend/`.** Human approved both the
  repo-wide scope and the worktree resync.

  The measurement that de-risked it, and that corrected my own earlier caution to the human:
  **nothing in this repo was ever STORED with CRLF.** `git ls-files --eol` showed all 504 tracked
  text files as `i/lf`. So the fix changes no committed content at all — proven, not assumed, by
  running `git add --renormalize .` and observing that the ONLY staged paths were the two ledger
  files being edited by hand at the time. I had told the human this would "renormalise stored
  files repo-wide"; that was wrong, and being wrong in the cautious direction still cost them a
  decision they did not need to agonise over.

  What was actually broken was the WORKTREE. `contracts/openapi.json`, `contracts/CONTRACT.lock`
  and `frontend/src/api/schema.d.ts` had `attr/` unspecified, and with `core.autocrlf=true` a
  fresh Windows clone hands all three out as CRLF — at which point ci.ps1 gate 10 (`Get-FileHash`)
  and `npm run check:api-drift` (string compare) both fail on line endings alone while
  ubuntu-latest stays green. Same class as TASK-0034, one layer out: a byte-exact gate is only as
  good as the bytes being reproducible. Root file mirrors `backend/.gitattributes`
  (`* text=auto eol=lf`, `*.cmd`/`*.bat` CRLF, binary list) and names the three generated
  artefacts explicitly so a future loosening of the blanket rule cannot silently take them.

  **Worktree resync:** 25 files (20 CRLF, 5 mixed) rewritten to LF — 19 of them EF migration
  scaffolds that were CRLF in the worktree DESPITE `backend/.gitattributes` already declaring
  `eol=lf`, because git only applies attributes on checkout and they had never been re-checked-out.
  Repo went 504→529 `i/lf w/lf`, zero CRLF, zero mixed; the 8 binaries and 1 empty file untouched.
  Verified content-identical four ways on a sample (`git ls-files -s`, `git hash-object --path`,
  `--no-filters`, and `rev-parse HEAD:`) — all four hashes equal.

  **Gotcha worth keeping:** after rewriting endings, `git status` kept reporting all 25 as `M`
  while `git diff` showed nothing, because the stat cache was stale and neither `git status` nor
  `git update-index --refresh` cleared it. `git update-index --really-refresh -- <paths>` did.
  `git checkout-index -f` is NOT a way to re-apply attributes to a stat-clean file — it silently
  does nothing — and the documented alternative (`git rm --cached -r . && git reset --hard`) would
  have destroyed uncommitted work, so it was not used.

  **Gates re-run after the change:** contract still 160697 bytes / `9dca7f11…` matching
  `CONTRACT.lock`; artefact-vs-contract hash match `true` (gate 10 equivalent); frontend
  `check:api-drift` — "No drift".

- 2026-09-07 **TASK-0034 closed — `contracts/openapi.json` was never reproducible across
  platforms, and gate 10's byte-exact hash was right to say so.** Roslyn writes the XML doc files
  (`bin/**/SchoolManagement.*.xml`) with `Environment.NewLine`, NOT the newline of the `.cs`
  source (every one of which is LF via `backend/.gitattributes`): measured 1937 CRLF / 0 LF in
  `SchoolManagement.Domain.xml`. `Microsoft.Extensions.ApiDescription.Server` lifts those
  `<summary>` bodies into `description` fields, so a Windows-promoted document carried **93
  escaped `\r\n` across 50 lines** — 160883 bytes / `73316bdb…` — while `ubuntu-latest` (both
  workflows) regenerated the same commit as 160697 bytes / `9dca7f11…`. Every CI run of a
  Windows-promoted contract was therefore red, on content that means nothing.

  **Fix:** `generate-openapi.ps1` normalises the escaped CRLF (and any lone escaped CR) to LF on
  the **generated artefact**, on every run, `-Promote` or not. Placement is the whole point: gate
  10 hashes `backend/artifacts/openapi/SchoolManagement.Api.json`, so normalising only inside the
  `-Promote` branch would have relocated the identical bug onto Windows workstations. Guarded by
  `OpenApiContractTests.NoDescription_ContainsACarriageReturn`, which walks every `description`
  recursively rather than trusting the 93 known offenders.

  **Verified non-breaking, not assumed:** parsing both revisions and normalising newlines in each
  makes them byte-identical; 21 paths, 38 schemas, key order unchanged; `git diff` on `contracts/`
  is 51+/51- with ZERO non-`description` lines touched. Frontend needed nothing —
  `openapi-typescript` already strips carriage returns, so `check:api-drift` stayed green and
  `schema.d.ts` was unchanged. Committed as `8a31384`.

  **Gates:** ALL TEN PASSED, `total=457 passed=457 failed=0 skipped=0`, line 80.19% / branch
  64.74%, gate 10 "The committed contract matches the code."

  **Costliest detour, worth not repeating:** two full runs failed on the hosted Postgres with
  DISJOINT failure sets (1 failure in 13 m, then 3 different ones in 41 m 42 s), all socket drops
  in fixture setup or a 500 where a 404 answers in milliseconds. Re-running those four filtered
  gave 4/4 in **23 s**; the clean full run then did 125/125 in 9 m 51 s. Same commit throughout —
  only the database's health differed. Separately, the first re-run attempt reported
  `Skipped: 125, Total: 125` with **exit code 0**, because a bare `dotnet test` never receives
  `POSTGRES_TEST_CONNECTION`: ci.ps1 resolves it via `lib/postgres-test-connection.ps1`
  (`Initialize-PostgresTestConnection -HomeDirectory $HOME` — the parameter is mandatory). That is
  exactly §13's false-pass shape. **Never verify a suite with a hand-rolled `dotnet test`; run
  ci.ps1, or dot-source that library and check the executed count.**

- 2026-09-07 **TASK-0033 — coordinator caught acceptance criterion 4 checked off with no test
  behind it in the entry immediately below; gap closed same session.** Criterion 4 reads "Prove
  the typed layer REJECTS an `idempotencyKey` on operations that do not declare it." The dispatch
  below only ever proved the other direction — `@ts-expect-error` on an OMITTED *required*
  `Idempotency-Key` (`CreateRole`) and on omitted required path params — and checked the box
  anyway, off the shape of the work rather than the literal wording of the criterion. Neither
  `client-roles.test.ts` nor `client.test.ts` had a REJECTS-an-extra-`idempotencyKey` case
  anywhere in the suite until this entry.

  **Fix**: two new `@ts-expect-error` cases in `client-roles.test.ts` — `GET /api/v1/privileges`
  (options type carries nothing beyond `signal`/`timeout`) and `GET /api/v1/roles` (options type
  is not trivially empty, so the rejection isn't just "an empty type rejects everything"). Read
  `IdempotencyKeyOf`/`RequestExtras` in `client-types.ts` first: both operations have
  `header?: never` (or no header position), so `IdempotencyKeyOf` resolves to `undefined` and the
  idempotency branch of `RequestExtras` contributes `unknown` — the options type ends up with no
  `idempotencyKey` property, so the rejection is TypeScript's ordinary excess-property check on a
  fresh object literal, not a bespoke mechanism. **Verified, not assumed**: removed each
  `@ts-expect-error` in turn, re-ran `npm run typecheck` — `TS2353: Object literal may only
  specify known properties, and 'idempotencyKey' does not exist in type 'Omit<CallerOptions,
  "params">'` at the privileges call site, identical `TS2353` at the roles call site — restored
  both, suite re-verified green. The type layer genuinely rejects it; this was a missing test, not
  a real looseness in the wrapper surface.

- 2026-09-07 **TASK-0028 dispatch 2 verified by orchestrator — the raw-SQL decision is sounder
  than its own justification.** `RoleRepository.ListAsync` splices three fragments into
  `SqlQueryRaw` as literal text (`sortColumn`, `comparisonOperator`, `orderDirection`), which is
  injection-shaped and was the one claim worth checking rather than accepting. The agent defended
  it on the `ListRolesQueryValidator` whitelist. **The real guarantee is stronger and does not
  depend on the validator at all:** `sortColumn` is one of two string literals chosen by an `==`
  comparison and the other two derive from a `bool`, so no caller text reaches SQL text even if
  the validator were removed or wrong. Every actual value stays a bound `{n}` parameter. Verified
  there are exactly three splice sites in the file and no others. Also verified: hash
  `73316bdb…` matches `CONTRACT.lock`, 21 paths with `/api/v1/roles` and `/api/v1/roles/{id}` new
  and nothing reshaped; the migration's only `DropTable` is in `Down()`, `Up()` is a `CreateTable`
  plus a `CreateIndex`. **Worth keeping as a habit:** a defence that rests on a validator is weaker
  than one that rests on the type of the input — when the two coincide, say which one you are
  relying on, because the validator can be edited by the next card and the `bool` cannot.
  Dispatch 2 was a continuation after a rate-limit cut-off; the six inherited files were kept, with
  two mechanical doc-comment fixes disclosed rather than made silently (a class-level `<remarks>`
  cannot use `<paramref>` — CS1734 under warnings-as-errors).

  **Application**: `Security/Roles/` gained `RoleMapper`, `CreateRole(+Handler)`, `UpdateRole(+Handler)`,
  `DeleteRole(+Handler)`, `GetRole(+Handler)`, `ListRoles(+Handler)`. Create/update build (or mutate)
  the entity FIRST — which validates the reserved name, field lengths and privilege-registry
  membership, naming an unknown code as the offender — and only THEN run
  `RolePrivilegeEscalationGuard.ValidateAddition`, so a genuinely unrecognised privilege code is
  never misreported as an escalation attempt. Update's escalation check snapshots
  `role.Privileges.ToArray()` BEFORE calling `SetPrivileges` (`Privileges` is a live view over the
  same backing list, so capturing it after mutation would silently defeat the "added privileges
  only" rule). A rejected escalation still mutates the tracked entity in memory, but the
  unit-of-work's roll-back-on-failure guarantee (AGENTS.md §4) means nothing is persisted — proven,
  not assumed, by `RoleEndpointsTests.Update_RuleTwo_...` reading the role back afterward.

- 2026-09-06 **TASK-0032 implemented by backend-dev — gate 9 now sees the working tree, and the
  six pre-existing findings are cleared; status → review.** Two problems, one card.

  **Problem 1 (six `generic-api-key` findings, TASK-0027's fake `temporaryPassword` example,
  commits `814b711`/`4170314`): cleared via a content-anchored `.gitleaks.toml` allowlist entry
  ("tighten the rule"), NOT a `.gitleaksignore` fingerprint and NOT changing the example string.**
  Full reasoning in `backend/docs/ASSUMPTIONS.md` §2.19; short version — a git-mode fingerprint
  (TASK-0017's mechanism) would only clear the pre-existing scan, and this card adds a SECOND scan
  of the same still-committed files, so the fingerprint would need maintaining twice against a
  mutable-content risk the ignore file's own header already warns against; changing the example
  string does not clear the two introducing commits at all (git-mode re-evaluates each commit's own
  diff regardless of current content) and would force a contract regeneration for zero benefit
  against a card declared `Contract impact: none`. New Family C in `.gitleaks.toml`, anchored to the
  literal `temporaryPassword": "aB3xQ9mK2pL7vN4wR8dT"`, `targetRules = ["generic-api-key"]`.

  **Problem 2 (the gate only ever scanned committed history, so it fired one dispatch late):**
  `ci.ps1`'s Secret scan gate now runs gitleaks TWICE — pass 1 unchanged (git history, whole repo
  via git's own root discovery despite `Push-Location backend/`); pass 2 new,
  `gitleaks detect --source <repo-root> --no-git --config .gitleaks.toml`, `--source` pointed at
  the repo root explicitly since `--no-git` has no git root to discover from on its own. Chose
  `--no-git` over `protect --staged`: staged-only would miss the ordinary shape of a dispatch's own
  working tree before `git add` ever runs. Gate fails if either pass fails.

  Turning on a whole-repo `--no-git` pass surfaced three things problem 1 didn't name, all

- 2026-09-06 **TASK-0029 implemented by frontend-dev — client regenerated, typed wrapper surface
  complete; status → review.** `npm run generate:api` re-run against the committed contract
  (`sha256 1a2d8afff15736c4f2b23894743b80de0d87f19ece7a70996db76a6eb8408926`, independently
  recomputed with `sha256sum` before starting) rewrote `src/api/schema.d.ts` — **+1037/-23 lines**,
  the five `/admins*` paths plus `lockedUntil` on `ProblemDetails`, generated header intact.
  *(ORCHESTRATOR CORRECTION 2026-09-06: the agent's own report and the first draft of this entry
  claimed the regeneration produced "zero diff" and that `check:api-drift`'s red state was
  `client.ts` being stale. Both are false and I verified so: `git diff --numstat` shows the 1037-line
  rewrite, and `check:api-drift` only ever compares `schema.d.ts` against the contract — `client.ts`
  is not an input to it. The WORK was right; only the narration was. Corrected here because a ledger
  that misstates which artefact was stale will mislead the next drift diagnosis.)*
  **`client.ts` split into two files** to stay under CONVENTIONS.md §3's 180-line cap (267
  lines combined would have exceeded it): `client.ts` (148 lines — the runtime verb helpers
  `apiGet`/`apiPost`/`apiPatch`/`apiDelete`) and new `client-types.ts` (139 lines — the
  type-level derivation: `SuccessBody`, `QueryOf`, `RequestBodyOf`, `PathParamsOf`,
  `IdempotencyKeyOf`, `RequestExtras`, `OptionsArgs`, …).
  **Path templating**: a `pathParams` field on each verb's trailing options argument,
  substituted into `{name}` segments at call time, type-driven from
  `operations[...]["parameters"]["path"]` — never a string the caller formats. The whole
  options argument becomes REQUIRED (not just the field) exactly when an operation declares a
  path parameter, via a generic `OptionsArgs<Op, Base>` rest-tuple type that also existing
  0/1-arg `apiGet`/`apiPost` call sites (`src/features/auth/api.ts`, the original
  `client.test.ts` tests) satisfy unchanged, since neither of those operations demands one.
  **`Idempotency-Key`** threaded through a dedicated `idempotencyKey` field (never folded into
  a generic headers bag — deliberately distinguished from `X-CSRF-Token` per the dispatch's
  explicit instruction): required exactly on `POST /admins`, optional on `PATCH /admins/{id}`,
  `POST /admins/{id}/status`, `POST /admins/{id}/password-reset` and `PATCH /settings/identity`,
  absent everywhere else — all derived generically from the header parameter's own
  required/optional-ness in the schema (`IdempotencyKeyOf<Op>`), not hardcoded per path.
  **`X-CSRF-Token` has no field anywhere in the typed surface**: `CallerOptions` is
  `Omit<RequestOptions, 'headers'>`, so `Idempotency-Key` is the only header a caller can ever
  set through `client.ts`; `http-client.ts`'s request interceptor (unmodified, still the
  `X-CSRF-Token` line in `attachAuthInterceptors`) keeps injecting CSRF on every mutating
  request regardless of what the schema's header parameter claims is "required".
  **`SuccessBody` gained a `204 → void` branch** (`deleteRequest`'s own `TResponse = void`
  default lines up), since `DELETE /admins/{id}/sessions` is this contract's first
  no-response-body operation and would otherwise resolve `never`.
  **Nine new `@ts-expect-error` type-level tests** in `client.test.ts` prove the enforcement
  rather than assert it: omitting `pathParams` on `apiGet`/`apiPatch`/`apiDelete` against
  `/admins/{id}` and `/admins/{id}/sessions` (both with the options argument absent and
  present-but-empty), and omitting `idempotencyKey` on `POST /admins`. **Every one verified
  genuinely erroring**, not vacuously accepted (§9's "a check that cannot be shown to fail is
  not a check") — caught a real methodology error along the way: the first verification attempt
  used `npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json` directly, which is a no-op against this repo's
  solution-style root `tsconfig.json` (`"files": []`, only `references`) and silently checks
  nothing; re-run the correct way (`npm run typecheck`, i.e. `tsc -b`, which follows the
  project references), removing each directive in turn produced exactly the expected compile
  error (`TS2554`/`TS2345`) at exactly that line, then every directive was restored and the
  suite re-verified green from a clean incremental-build cache.
  **Deliberate, dispatch-directed exception to CONVENTIONS.md §2's "no `@ts-expect-error`"**:
  the orchestrator's dispatch explicitly named it "the standard way" to prove a
  required-parameter omission fails typecheck; used for exactly that, nowhere else in the tree.

  and before verifying leaves a working tree that looks finished and is not, and the tell is
  silence.* Everything below was re-run or re-read locally, not accepted on report: build 0
  warnings / 0 errors (Release); `dotnet format --verify-no-changes` exit 0; **unit 249, arch 28,

  packages; `gitleaks detect --source . --config .gitleaks.toml` no leaks; and §4.4 check 1 run by

  `me.refetch()`, not a raw call). §4.4 check 2 is knowingly stale and owned by **TASK-0029**.
  **The reusable finding, worth more than the card:** `ConcurrentDuplicatePosts_…` had asserted a
  SCHEDULING ACCIDENT since the day it was written — it demanded `[Created, Conflict]`, but two
  same-key requests have two legal shapes, and which one occurs depends on whether the winner
  completes before the loser reads. Sixteen instrumented runs settled it: fifteen produced
  `[Created, Conflict]`, one (under full-suite load against hosted Neon) produced
  `[Created, Created]` with the second response carrying `Idempotency-Replay: true` — **and a row
  count of exactly 1 in every one of the sixteen.** The substrate never double-executed; the test
  was wrong, not TASK-0019. It now accepts either shape, additionally asserts the replayed body
  matches the winner's verbatim, and keeps the exactly-one-side-effect count unconditional. A test
  that names an accident in its assertion will fail the day the timing changes, and will look like
  a product defect when it does.
- 2026-09-06 **TASK-0027 REVIEWED by orchestrator — REOPENED on three gaps; dispatch 1 otherwise
  stands.** What was verified independently rather than taken on report, all green: `CONTRACT.lock`
  matches the document byte for byte (`e434db40…`, recomputed); `Idempotency-Replay` is declared BY
  CONSTRUCTION via `IdempotencyHeaderOperationTransformer`, not hand-annotated (the defect TASK-0003
  was reopened for did not recur); the rate-limiter ordering fix is real and its new test could NOT
  pass under IP partitioning (two accounts, one client, independent budgets); the super-admin
  invariant is a genuine `FOR UPDATE` row lock, not a pre-flight read, with a concurrency test; the
  redaction test reads the stored idempotency row back out of Postgres and asserts `temporaryPassword`
  is JSON null; the TASK-0028 seam is an explicit `TODO(TASK-0028)` at the deactivation site.
  **The three gaps** — (1) "session tokens rotate on privilege change … **prove it fires**" was
  checked off with NO test reaching `UpdateAdminAccountHandler.cs:176`: both `isSuperAdmin` tests are
  rejection paths that never execute the rotation, and the domain test `SetSuperAdmin_FlipsTheFlag…`
  does not touch sessions. (2) The email-uniqueness criterion's second half — a deactivated account's
  email is reusable — is correctly IMPLEMENTED (`EmailExistsActiveOrSuspendedAsync` excludes
  `Deactivated`) but has no test. (3) The `lockedUntil` / `additionalProperties: false` drift entry
  names TASK-0027 as its trigger and is untouched. **The standing lesson this sharpens:** gate output
  proves the suite that exists is green; it says nothing about whether a criterion's test was ever
  written, and a checked box is the agent's claim, not evidence. Criteria whose wording is "prove it"
  need the assertion located during review, not the checkbox counted.

## 2026-09-14 — TASK-0041, 0042, 0043 and 0045 closed on one orchestrator gate run

Four frontend cards closed together. 0041/0042/0043 had sat in `review` since 2026-09-08;
0045 had never been recorded as dispatched at all. All four ship in commit `6f3a807`
("feat(frontend): back-office shell and the configuration screens"), 103 files, 8,541
insertions.

### TASK-0045 was already implemented, and the ledger said "queued"

**This is the finding that mattered most, and it is the one a closure review exists to catch.**
The 2026-09-14 reconciliation pass had just rediscovered TASK-0045 — absent from `## In flight`,
referenced by no other file in the repo — and reinstated it as *"queued, card is written in full
and dispatchable"*. It was not queued. `frontend/src/features/arms/` is complete: all seven
operations, its own route slice, a nav entry gated on `arm.view`, 1,800-odd lines with dedicated
tests, and `e2e/arms.spec.ts`. The code cites the card by name in eight places
(`arm-list.test.tsx:85` — "displayName is always the server value (TASK-0045 AC)").

Some dispatch implemented it against the card and never updated `Status:`, never wrote a `## Log`
line, and never appended to the ledger. The reconciliation pass then found the card orphaned and
drew the only inference available from the card header — that it had not been started.

**The lesson, and it is a sharper version of 2026-09-14's own "closure is real only where a
closure entry exists":** that rule was derived from three cards whose headers over-claimed
(`done` with no closure entry). TASK-0045 is the same failure in the opposite direction — a
header that *under*-claimed — and the reconciliation had no way to see it, because it compared
card headers against the archive and never against the working tree. **A card's status is a claim
about the tree, and only the tree can refute it.** Reconciliation must grep the code for the
card's own id before believing `Status: queued`. Had "run the next task" been taken at face value
here, the next dispatch would have rebuilt ~1,800 lines of working, tested code on top of itself.

### Review against the acceptance criteria

Checked from the diff and the contract, not from the agents' reports:

- **`displayName` verbatim from the server** (`arm-list.tsx:74`), never recomposed from level +
  label. This was the AC most likely to be quietly violated and the one with the worst failure
  mode (a result sheet reading "Primary 1 A").
- **No client-side re-sort** of `GET /arms` (`arms/api.ts:29`), with the contrast to `useLevels`
  — which *does* re-sort, because its endpoint makes no ordering promise — documented in place.
- **Capacity rendered alone, no fabricated `0` enrolled.** Confirmed honest against the contract:
  `jq '.components.schemas.ArmDto.properties | keys'` carries no `enrolled` member.
- **Bulk create dry-runs first by construction** — no commit control exists until a preview is in
  state, and any edit to an input clears it. Proven falsifiably in `e2e/arms.spec.ts:143,152` by
  asserting the exact request sequence `[{dryRun:true},{dryRun:false}]`, not merely that a
  preview rendered.
- **Sign-out is server-first** — `apiPost` in `mutationFn`, `terminateSession()` in `onSuccess`
  (`features/auth/api.ts:34-44`). §5 blocker cleared.
- **No raw `fetch`/`axios`** outside `src/api/` and `src/lib/http/` (§4.4 check 3), by grep.
- **Idempotency keys** on all six create paths, generated per submit inside `mutationFn`, each
  submit control disabled while pending so a double-click cannot mint a second key.
- **Levels render in `progressionOrder`**, not alphabetically (`level-list.tsx:50`).
- No `TODO`/`FIXME` without a task number anywhere in `src/` or `e2e/` (DoD #4).

`UpdateArmCommand` requiring `id` in the *body* as well as the path looked like a defect on
reading `useUpdateArm`; `jq` of the schema confirms the contract genuinely requires it. Recorded
because the next reader will have the same doubt.

### Gates — run by the orchestrator, not accepted on report

```
npm run verify          Test Files 49 passed (49) · Tests 350 passed (350) · Skipped 0
                        typecheck 0 · lint 0 warnings · vite build 537 modules, exit 0
npm run check:api-drift  src/api/schema.d.ts matches contracts/openapi.json. No drift.
npm run test:e2e         9 passed (27.5s), Skipped 0
gitleaks --no-git        no leaks found (~6.99 MB scanned)
```

Contract unmoved: `7a3c84e6…`, 47 paths, 86 schemas. No card touched `contracts/**` or `src/api/**`.

### Drift resolved

**`src/shared/` is not a violation.** It looked like one — `STATE.md ## Layout` read "shared/
deliberately absent" — but `CONVENTIONS.md:126-130` says `src/shared/` "appears in the commit
that promotes the first thing genuinely shared across features, never in advance", and
`shared/forms/field-message.ts` now has thirteen cross-feature importers. TASK-0041's log shows
the agent correctly *declining* to create it ("this dispatch is not that commit") and a later
dispatch making the promotion once the second consumer appeared. The convention worked exactly as
written; the `## Layout` line simply went stale the moment it fired, and is corrected.

**The session-end redirect is now subscribed once, not per-screen** (drift 2026-09-06, trigger
"the next routing card" — that was TASK-0041). `protected-layout.tsx:28` is the sole production
subscriber; every other `onSessionEnded` hit in the repo is a test. Struck.

### Drift NOT resolved, and a card-authoring miss on the orchestrator

**Spec 6.1.2's self-edit carve-out** (an admin editing their own `staffName`/`phone` without
holding `admin.update`) remains unbuilt. Its drift trigger was *"the next `/admins` card"* —
TASK-0043 is that card, and **no acceptance criterion on it named the carve-out.** This is
precisely the failure `rules/governance.md` §3 row 4 and its mechanical grep exist to prevent,
and it happened on a card I wrote before that check existed.

What limited the damage is the other half of §3: the implementing agent flagged it anyway rather
than silently shipping around it — in `admins/api.ts:77` and as "Deviation 2" in the card's log.
The entry stays live, with its trigger rewritten to something that cannot arrive unnoticed, and
its owner corrected to `backend-dev` first: the route must accept the narrower authorisation
before any screen can offer it.

## 2026-09-14 (TASK-0053) — CSV formula injection neutralised in the audit export; closed first-run

**Closed on one full green `ci.ps1`:** 10/10 gates, `Tests: total=917 passed=917 failed=0
skipped=0`, line 81.52% / branch 69.61%. Contract hash unmoved at `7a3c84e6…` (gate 10 PASS) —
the card's own last criterion, since an encoding fix must not widen the document.

`AuditEventCsvWriter.Neutralize` prefixes a single apostrophe to any field whose first character is
`=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, TAB or CR, wired as `Escape(Neutralize(field))`. 27 production lines against the
card's 60-line ceiling. The order is the whole substance: neutralise first, then apply TASK-0049's
RFC 4180 quoting to the result. Reversed, the apostrophe lands outside the quotes and a field
containing a comma gains a column. The CR case is the one input where both mechanisms fire, and it
has a dedicated test for exactly that reason.

Applied to **every** field rather than the three obviously-attacker-reachable ones. `userAgent` is
the live path (captured verbatim from the request header, and failed sign-ins are audited, so an
unauthenticated attacker sets it), but enumerating "the dangerous columns" is how the next one gets
missed, and uniform application is cheap. The blanket `-` prefix rests on a stated assumption —
none of the thirteen columns currently emits a signed number — recorded in the code comment so a
future signed column is not silently mangled.

Eleven tests: one per dangerous leading character, an ordinary-field control, two proving TASK-0049's
RFC 4180 behaviour is unchanged, one proving uniform application across five columns, and an
integration test seeding `user_agent = =HYPERLINK("http://evil","x")` and asserting on the real
response body. That last one carries a negative assertion (`ShouldNotContain(",=HYPERLINK(")`) so it
would have gone red before the fix — the standing "a check that cannot be shown to fail is not a
check" rule, satisfied deliberately.

### The process cost, which was entirely the orchestrator's

The integration suite appeared to hang for 30 minutes and **the orchestrator killed it**. That was
premature, and the kill landed mid `ResetDatabaseAsync`, leaving the shared Neon database in the
exact `23505`-plus-contradictory-`23503` state `rules/gates.md` section 4 documents. Roughly two
hours went into diagnosing damage that the diagnosis itself had caused.

Two wrong hypotheses were published to the human before the right reading: first that an orphaned
session was blocking `TRUNCATE` (disproved — `pg_locks` showed no lock held by any other session,
and a probe acquired the lock immediately), then that the suite was merely slow and still
progressing (half-right).

**What is actually true, and is the reusable lesson:** `ci.ps1` runs `dotnet test` WITHOUT a console
logger, so it prints nothing between "test run for" and the final summary. `ResetDatabaseAsync` runs
per test — a `TRUNCATE` of every table plus three reseeds — and against hosted Neon rather than a
local container that costs ~8 seconds per test. 256 integration tests therefore produce **35+
minutes of complete silence that is indistinguishable from a hang.** Re-running with
`--logger "console;verbosity=normal"` showed steady per-test progress and the suite completed
256/256, exit 0. The full gate then passed 917/917 untouched.

**Standing rule this produces: silence from `ci.ps1`'s integration stage is not evidence of a hang.
Before killing a gate run, get evidence it is actually stuck** — `pg_stat_activity` on the test
database (a changing `query` and a small `state_change` delta means progress), or re-run the suite
alone with a console logger. Killing a run mid-reseed costs more than waiting out a slow one.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0059) — Enrolment: the pupil-to-arm spine, closed first-run

**Closed on one full green `ci.ps1`:** 10/10 gates, `Tests: total=933 passed=933 failed=0
skipped=0`, line 81.63% / branch 69.84%. Contract hash unmoved at `7a3c84e6…` — the card added no
route, by design.

`enrolment` joins one pupil to one arm with `effective_from` and a nullable `effective_to`, per
`02-data-model.md` section 5.2. Open, Close and Transfer are domain operations; Transfer closes the
current row and opens a new one rather than mutating `arm_id`, because the history of who sat where
is the product requirement, not a nicety.

**The invariant is enforced in the DATABASE**, not only the domain: a partial unique index,
`unique: true, filter: "effective_to IS NULL"`, so a pupil can have exactly one open enrolment.
Proven in both directions — a second open row violates it, and a second row succeeds once the first
is closed. **The second test is the load-bearing one**: without it a blanket unique index would pass
the first test and silently break every transfer in the product.

`pupil` carries no `arm_id` or `class_level_id`, guarded by a test that queries `information_schema`
directly. Section 5.2 calls this "the single most important normalisation in the schema" and warns
the shortcut "will break transfers, promotion and historical results at the same time", so the guard
asserts against the real catalogue rather than the EF model.

**Drift closed:** arm-scoped `pupil.view`/`pupil.update` was a tested mechanism against data that
could not exercise it; `PupilArmOfRecordLookup` now resolves the arm from the open enrolment and the
scope check bites. The throwing stand-in is deleted.

**Scope reduced by one AC, deliberately, at the pre-authorised cut line.** The `GET /pupils`
class-progression sort went to TASK-0061 because 6.5.15 defines the order for pupils who are in a
class and is silent on pupils with no open enrolment — which `transferred`, `withdrawn`, `graduated`
and `pending` records all are. Four answers are defensible and visibly different. The agent stopped
and asked instead of inventing product behaviour. **A card that comes back one AC short with the
question written down is a better outcome than one that comes back complete and guessed.**

**A stale pointer handled correctly.** `PrivilegeDecision.cs` carried `TODO(TASK-0002)` against a
closed card. The agent refused to retarget it to a number nothing had opened, tagged it and
escalated. The orchestrator opened TASK-0060 and tagged the source. The concern itself is real and
worth stating plainly: `PrivilegeDecision` ignores `PrivilegeGrant.SessionId`, so **a grant scoped to
one academic session authorises the same action against a target in another.** Implementing the
lookup did not fix it, because pupil routes bypass `ScopeResolver` via the handler-level
`PupilAccessGuard`. Cross-cutting, and it gets worse once results and multiple live sessions exist.

**~550 hand-written production lines against the ~400 guideline**, declared by the agent rather than
found in review, and accepted: the overrun is the arm-scope wiring the drift-closing AC required.

### Reading a pooled test database — the orchestrator got this wrong twice

While gating, `pg_stat_activity` was sampled once, showed a session `idle` for ~4 minutes, and was
read as a stall in the auth/lockout path. That was reported to the human and was **wrong**. The test
connection is a Neon **pooler** endpoint: `pg_stat_activity` shows pooled connections under
different backend pids, and one sitting idle is simply a connection in the pool, not the worker. A
second sample showed a different pid, a sub-second `state_change`, and `class_levels` advanced from
1 to 9.

Combined with the TASK-0053 lesson (`ci.ps1` logs nothing until its final summary), the rule is:
**judge a running gate by TWO samples showing a changed session or advancing row counts — never by
one session's idle time, and never by the absence of console output.** Both readings that broke this
rule produced a wrong conclusion, and the first one also produced a killed run and a corrupted
database.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0061) — Human ruling: a pupil with no open enrolment sorts LAST

**The question, as TASK-0059's implementing agent left it:** 6.5.15 defines the register's default
sort as class-in-progression-order then surname, and says nothing about a pupil who is in no class.
The agent stopped rather than invent product behaviour, which is what `CLAUDE.md` section 1 asks for.

**The ruling: option A — last, as one flat trailing block, ordered surname then id.** Given by the
human on 2026-09-15 against the four options below.

### What the question actually covered, once the tree was read

Two repo facts narrowed it before the options were drawn, and both belong in the card:

1. **`pending` was never in scope.** TASK-0050's model-level EF query filter
   (`PupilConfiguration.HasQueryFilter(p => p.Status != PupilStatus.Pending)`) already excludes
   pending pupils from the register entirely. The ruling therefore governs **`transferred`,
   `withdrawn` and `graduated`** — plus the one genuinely never-enrolled record that survives the
   filter as `withdrawn`: the `pending → withdrawn` lapsed application of 6.5.14, which is issued no
   registration number and opens no enrolment.
2. **The cursor is `(surnameKey, id)`** — `PupilListCursor.Encode`. Any option putting a NULLABLE
   level or arm into the sort key puts a NULL into the keyset comparison, which is exactly the page
   seam the card's across-a-boundary criterion exists to catch.

### The options, and why the other three lost

| | Ruling | Why not |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | **Last**, one trailing block, surname then id | **chosen** |
| B | First, before all classes | Same cost as A; the register's first page becomes the people who have left. Worse on the screen the office uses daily |
| C | Excluded from the default list, reachable only via `status=` | **Breaks search.** 6.5.15's search runs through this same list query, so a gate officer or the office looking up a transferred pupil would get nothing. It also invents a second invisible exclusion invariant beside pending — a visibility change wearing an ordering change's clothes |
| D | Sorted by the most recent CLOSED enrolment, so a graduated pupil sits inside Primary 6 | **Does not answer the question**: the `pending → withdrawn` record has no enrolment of any kind, so D still needs A or B underneath it. It is also the most expensive and the highest cursor risk — a correlated latest-closed-enrolment subquery, a derived value in the cursor, ties where two closed enrolments share a date, and a key that jumps on the 6.5.14 reactivation transition |

**Why A wins on its own merits, not just by elimination:** it matches what 6.5.15 says the sort is
FOR — "the order a school reads a register in" — and a physical register has current classes in
progression order with leavers at the back, not interleaved. It is also the only option that is
purely an ordering ruling: C changes visibility, D changes visibility semantics and needs a
fallback anyway. A answers the question completely and moves nothing else.

### Two riders, binding on the implementation

- **No sub-grouping by status inside the trailing block.** Do not order transferred, then withdrawn,
  then graduated. 6.5.15's `status` column already carries that, and every extra sort key is another
  cursor field and another seam to get wrong.
- **The sentinel is the point.** Implement the trailing position with a NON-NULL sentinel sort value
  (a max level ordinal, an empty arm key), so the widened keyset `(levelOrdinal, armOrdinal,
  surnameKey, id)` stays a straight tuple comparison and the cursor never carries a NULL. This is
  what keeps the seam test deterministic.
- **If the real want is leavers off the register, the lever is a default `status` filter, not sort
  position.** That is contract-visible and needs its own card. It is explicitly NOT smuggled in here.

**Standing lesson, and the reason this entry is longer than the ruling:** the agent returned
TASK-0059 one acceptance criterion short with the open question written down, rather than complete
and guessed. Reconstructing the question cost one read of 6.5.14/6.5.15 and two greps. Reconstructing
a wrong guess would have cost a migration, a cursor change and a re-gate. This is the second entry in
two days recording the same trade, and it keeps being worth it.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0051) — Written in full, and split three ways on an unbuilt dependency

**The finding, and it is the reason this entry exists:** TASK-0051 was the next critical-path card,
and `STATE.md` had recorded it on 2026-09-15 as "**UNBLOCKED** — every dependency now cleared
(0005c, 0050, enrolment)". That was wrong. Writing the card in full meant reading 6.5.10 and 6.5.11
against the tree, and the tree has **no `admission_record` entity at all** — no
`Domain/Pupils/AdmissionRecord.cs`, nothing in the DbContext, and `CreatePupilCommand` captures not
one section A field.

6.5.10 says the registration number's year comes from `admission_record.date_admitted`, and 6.5.11's
approval reads `class_admitted_into`, `assessment_required` and the section I declaration. **None of
those columns exist.** The dependency was absent from TASK-0051's own `Depends on:` line (which named
0005c, 0050 and enrolment), from the `## In flight` row, and from `ROADMAP.md`. Four places recorded
the card as ready; none of them was checking the tree.

This is the second time in two days that `rules/governance.md` section 1's "grep the TREE, not just
the archive" has paid, and the first time it caught an **over**-claiming ledger row rather than an
under-claiming card header. The check as written says to grep for the card's id and the paths its
scope names. TASK-0051's scope names `admission_record`; one `ls` of the domain folder settled it.

### The split

| Card | Scope | Why it is its own card |
|---|---|---|
| **TASK-0062** | `admission_record`: 6.5.9's sections A, I and J, created in the same transaction as the pupil; `PATCH /admissions/{id}`; the queue's `missing` column | The dependency. Entity + configuration + migration + repository + handler wiring is the exact shape TASK-0059 shipped three days ago, and is a card on its own |
| **TASK-0051** (narrowed) | Approve, decline, the atomic counter increment, the number, the first enrolment, idempotency | Still the largest of the three. Ten acceptance criteria, four of them concurrency or transaction boundaries |
| **TASK-0063** | Correction plus `pupil_reg_number_history` as a permanent lookup alias | A separate transaction against a separate table under a different privilege (`pupil.regnumber.correct`, Super Admin). Folding it in pushes 0051 well past the ~400-line guideline |

**What did NOT get invented to make this fit.** The tempting shortcut was to take `dateAdmitted` in
the approve payload and skip `admission_record` entirely. That contradicts 6.5.9 (one row per pupil,
resumable from step 1) and 6.5.11 (a resumable multi-step record, not a single submission), and it
would have had to be unpicked by the admission-flow card. `CLAUDE.md` section 1 forbids inventing
product behaviour; inventing a data model to dodge a missing one is the same move.

### Two gaps recorded rather than papered over

1. **The completeness gate cannot be complete.** 6.5.11 blocks approval when "any required item
   across steps 1 to 8 is missing", but steps 3 to 8's entities — contacts, health, barred persons,
   pickup persons, documents — are unbuilt. Both 0062 and 0051 therefore cover sections A, B, I and
   J only, say so in `## Out of scope`, and require the implementing agent to name the unchecked
   sections in its closing note.
2. **`POST /admissions` vs `POST /pupils`.** 6.5.17 calls step 1 `POST /admissions`; TASK-0050 built
   it as `POST /pupils`. TASK-0062 extends the existing route — renaming is BREAKING and needs its
   own card. Written into 0062's notes as an explicit do-not-fix, because it is exactly the kind of
   tidy-up an agent does unasked.

**Also cleared while here:** the 2026-09-06 drift entry for `abbreviation.issuedCount` (ships
`null`, "trigger: the reg-number issue card") now names TASK-0051 by id, and 0051 carries it as an
acceptance criterion — with the note that the count reads the pupil table, not the counter, so a
declined or corrected record cannot inflate it.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0062) — `admission_record` closed, and a contract delta the orchestrator misclassified

**Closed on a full green `ci.ps1`:** 10/10 gates, 953 tests (651 unit, 33 architecture, 269
integration), `Failed 0 / Skipped 0`, line 81.25% / branch 69.40%. Contract hash moved
`7a3c84e6…` → `de4397b4164d…`. Frontend client regenerated and `check:api-drift` green.

### The orchestrator classified a BREAKING contract delta as additive

TASK-0062's card said "`POST /pupils` — request gains a REQUIRED `admission` object" and the same
card's header said `Contract impact: **additive**`. The orchestrator approved it on that basis and
dispatched without human sign-off. **Both statements were in the same card, four lines apart, and
the contradiction was not noticed.**

`rules/contract.md` section 2 lists "making an optional field required" as breaking. A NEW required
request field is not word-for-word on that list, but it is the same thing from a caller's side: a
request that worked yesterday is rejected today. `contract-guardian` caught it on the closing drift
check — `admission` is present in `CreatePupilCommand`'s `required` array and was absent before.

**Human sign-off obtained 2026-09-15, after the fact, and the change stands as shipped.** The
reasoning accepted: no non-test caller of `POST /pupils` exists (the frontend has client tests and
no pupils screens, per TASK-0052's closure), so no real caller breaks; and the alternative —
`admission` optional — would permit a pupil with no admission record, which is precisely the state
this card was written to make unreachable and would leave 6.5.10's registration-number year with
nothing to read at approval.

**The lesson, and it is about the check and not the classification:** the breaking-vs-additive call
is made when the card is WRITTEN, by the same agent that writes the delta prose, and nothing
re-reads it before dispatch. The `Contract impact:` header is a claim about the delta section three
lines below it, and those two can disagree silently. `contract-guardian`'s check 6 caught it — but
that check runs at CLOSE, which is after implementation, after the gate, and after the point where
a human sign-off could have changed anything. **Adding a required request field belongs on
section 2's breaking list explicitly**, so the next card cannot reach the same conclusion honestly.

### A defect the full gate caught and no filtered run could

The first `ci.ps1` failed 7 tests, all in `PipelineTests`, all one cause: `IAdmissionRecordRepository`
is registered in `InfrastructureDependencyInjection`, but `PipelineTests` builds a container from
the **Application assembly alone** and validates every handler in it, so `CreatePupilHandler` and
`UpdateAdmissionRecordHandler` could not resolve their new dependency.
`EveryRequestInTheAssemblyResolvesAHandler` exists to catch exactly this and did.

The implementing agent's own runs were honest and green — `--filter Admissions` 13/13, architecture
33/33, integration 28/28, `Skipped 0` throughout. **None of those filters selects `PipelineTests`.**
This is `rules/gates.md` section 1 working as designed rather than an agent being careless: the
subagent runs filtered suites, the orchestrator runs the full gate, and cross-cutting container
wiring only surfaces in the second. The card was reopened per section 5 rather than fixed by the
orchestrator; the fix was five lines in the fixture beside the identical `IPupilRepository` and
`IAuditEventQueryRepository` stubs, with no production code, no handler constructor and no test
excluded from the container check.

### Size, again

~1,700 hand-written lines against the ~400 guideline — declared by the agent, unprompted, with the
correct cut named: (a) entity, configuration, migration, repository, DI; (b) the product-facing
surface. TASK-0059 overran the same way three days ago (~550). **Two consecutive cards over the
guideline makes it a pattern in how the orchestrator writes cards, not a property of the work.** The
card was written as one unit, so it was implemented as one unit; the split had to exist before
dispatch to have any effect.

### Three declared gaps, all filed

Steps 3-to-8 invisible to the queue's `missing`; no settings default for `headOfSchoolName` (the
one genuine spec deviation of the three); `admission` null on `GET /pupils/{id}`. Full text and
triggers: `drift/2026-Q3.md`, same date. All three were declared in the agent's report rather than
found in review.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0051) — Registration number issue and the admission-approval transition, closed

**Gate:** `ci.ps1 -NoFailFast`, 8/10 PASS. 985 tests, `Failed 5 / Skipped 0`, line 81.52% /
branch 69.80%. The five were triaged, not waved through: **two were a real regression this card
introduced** (pre-existing `SettingsEndpointsTests` asserting `IssuedCount` is `null`, which this
card deliberately made a live count — fixed to `ShouldBe(0)` and re-run green, 32/32), and **three
were hosted-Neon connection drops** (`SocketException: An existing connection was forcibly closed
by the remote host`), re-run individually to prove it rather than asserted. Contract hash moved
`de4397b4164d…` → `4b31521d833b…`, 48 → 50 paths, 90 → 92 schemas.

### Two orchestrator rulings, both flagged to the human as rulings

Spec 6.5.9 says `head_of_school_name` "Defaults from settings" without naming the setting. The
orchestrator ruled the source to be `settings.head_teacher_name` — the only field of that shape in
6.2.3 — and pinned `headOfSchoolConfirmed` as required-and-true against appendix B question 26,
whose right-hand column is the stated default the build follows absent an answer. Both are
one-line reversals if the school says otherwise. **Neither was invented: each resolves a pointer
with exactly one referent, which is different from inventing product behaviour.**

### The pre-dispatch check caught two drift entries the card had not carried

`rules/governance.md` section 3 row 4's mechanical grep found that TWO live drift entries name
TASK-0051 as their trigger and neither had been written into the card at its full write-up the
same morning: `headOfSchoolName`'s missing settings default, and the null `admission` block on
pupil read paths. The first became an acceptance criterion, the second a note. **The card had been
written in full the same day by the same orchestrator, and still missed them** — which is the
argument for the grep being mechanical rather than remembered.

### The additive classification was verified mechanically, after TASK-0062

Given the previous card shipped a BREAKING delta under an `additive` header, this one was checked
by comparing every existing schema's `required` array and every property's type against HEAD,
rather than by reading the card. Result: two new paths, two new schemas, no existing `required`
array changed, no existing property type changed. The single change to an existing schema is
`SettingsAbbreviationGroupDto.issuedCount`'s **description** — documentation, not shape.
**The orchestrator's first comparison script was faulty and reported zero changed schemas,
including that description edit; the implementing agent's report was more accurate.** The verdict
survived the corrected check, but the first method did not.

### Three interruptions, one of them the orchestrator's own error

The dispatch died twice on session limits (the 4th and 5th such deaths on this project — 0003,
0019, 0005a, 0051 twice) and once on a **concurrency collision the orchestrator caused**: it
launched `ci.ps1` without first standing the agent down, the resumed agent started its own
integration run against the same shared Neon database, and `rules/gates.md` section 4 is explicit
that two concurrent runs corrupt it. **The orchestrator stopped its OWN gate rather than the
agent's** — its gate was at the architecture-test stage touching no database, whereas killing the
agent's run mid-reseed is precisely what corrupted this database on 2026-09-14. Cost: one full
gate run.

**Standing lesson: a resumed agent does not know what the orchestrator started while it was down.**
Stand the agent down BEFORE starting a gate, not after discovering the collision.

### Flaky-vs-real triage was settled by two samples, never one

Three separate runs each failed a DIFFERENT single test, each preceded by an outsized duration.
The implementing agent argued transience from that pattern; the orchestrator declined to accept it
on one run and waited for its own. The contested test (`Approve_TheYearComesFromDateAdmitted_
NeverFromToday`) passed on the orchestrator's run while a different one dropped — confirming the
agent's read. **Acting on either run alone would have sent someone chasing a phantom bug.**

### Deviations accepted

- The retry loop calls `IUnitOfWork.SaveChangesAsync` directly from the handler, departing from
  "handlers never call SaveChangesAsync". `IUnitOfWork`'s own remarks name this the sanctioned
  escape hatch, and EF Core's automatic SAVEPOINT under a caller-managed transaction makes a failed
  attempt roll back only itself. Reviewed and accepted.
- A genuine backstop collision burns one serial rather than reusing it, since the counter increment
  commits as its own statement before the savepoint exists. Accepted over hand-rolled savepoints
  for a path this unlikely to fire.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0064, TASK-0066)

**The admissions queue and approve/decline screen shipped, and the contract gap it exposed was
closed mid-flight.** TASK-0064 began as a one-line ledger placeholder reading "regenerate the API
client against `4b31521d…`". That is a generated file and zero product, so it was written up as the
screen instead: `rules/contract.md` §3 already pairs regeneration with consumption in ONE frontend
step, so the two belong in the same card rather than in two dispatches.

**`frontend-dev` refused to guess, and that refusal produced the endpoint.** The approve dialog's
arm selector needs `GET /arms?sessionId=&levelId=`, and neither id was reachable: the queue row
carries `levelAppliedFor`, a display NAME, and `admission` is null on every pupil read path. The
agent rejected two shims — name-matching against `GET /levels`, and assuming "the active session" —
and shipped a blocked stub naming the gap. Both shims would have compiled, passed their tests, and
admitted pupils into the wrong class on a name collision. This is `rules/contract.md` §5's "backend
wins the how, frontend wins the what" behaving exactly as written, and it is worth recording as the
cheap outcome: one extra backend card instead of a silent data defect.

**TASK-0066** is that card — `GET /admissions/{id}`, a read twin of `PATCH /admissions/{id}`. It
reuses `AdmissionRecordMapper` and the pending-only-404 rule rather than restating them, is
`AsNoTracking`, writes nothing, and is school-wide `pupil.view` only. The privilege was deliberately
NOT made arm-scoped like `GET /pupils`: on a list, an arm-scoped caller gets an honest empty page,
but on a single-record read "empty" is a 404 that lies about existence.

**Contract:** `4b31521d833b…` → `37f8b4c2d19db1af9c5adc0be1f002840cb667416e9072dc4aa74fc6818a2b53`.
Additive, verified mechanically by the orchestrator and not read off the card: exactly one `get`
method added to an already-existing path, zero schemas added or removed, and every existing
schema's `required` array and every property's type/format/$ref/enum byte-identical. `CONTRACT.lock`
matches the document hash.

**Gate: 10/10 PASS, 993 tests, Skipped 0** — 662 unit, 33 architecture, 298 integration (49m25s).
No triage, no re-runs. This is the first clean gate in several cards; TASK-0051's cost three runs.

**Two process notes, both orchestrator-side.** First, `backend-dev` ended a turn with an integration
run still executing, which blocks everything else because concurrent runs corrupt the shared Neon
database; it was resumed only after the run was confirmed drained, and the instruction has been
sharpened to "when you finish a turn, be finished". Second, the backend gate was run CONCURRENTLY
with the frontend agent's work — deliberately, and safely, because frontend work touches no
database. That is the correct read of `gates.md` §4, which forbids two concurrent DB runs, not all
concurrency; getting it wrong in the cautious direction on TASK-0051 cost a full 45-minute run.

**`backend-dev` verified additive against `git show HEAD:`**, which `rules/contract.md` §4 warns
against. It was valid here only because the tree happened to be clean at dispatch. Re-verified
against the actual pre-change document rather than left standing on that coincidence.

## 2026-09-15 (TASK-0063) — Registration-number correction and the permanent history alias, closed

A Super Admin corrects a wrongly-issued registration number by typing the replacement in full;
the old number survives permanently in `pupil_reg_number_history` as a lookup alias. Spec 6.5.10.

**The card was pre-ruled at dispatch rather than left open.** Four things the implementing agent
would otherwise have had to compose were decided by the orchestrator first and written into the
card: 200 returns the existing `PupilDto` (so the schema count moves by one, not two), the 409 code
is `pupil.registration_number_duplicate` (matching the repo's `<entity>.<field>_duplicate`
convention), the status set matches `approve`'s, and `pupil.regnumber.correct` already existed at
`Privileges.cs:230` and must not be re-added or widened. Each of those is a guess that costs a
review round trip; the card's `Notes` also named `ApproveAdmissionHandler.cs` and
`PupilEndpoints.cs` by path. The card came back needing no shape corrections at all.

**Contract:** `37f8b4c2d19d…` → `b293db2bc2b4fd86044cf2ec727051ab911920b71c4361e8d9dc6d0d379d8202`.
Additive: 50 → 51 paths, 92 → 93 schemas (`CorrectRegistrationNumberCommand` only).
`CONTRACT.lock` matches. Gate 10's OpenAPI drift check passed, which is what independently proves
the committed document is what the backend actually emits.

**Gate: 1035 tests, Skipped 0, coverage 81.75% line / 69.90% branch.** The `ci.ps1` run came back
9/10 with one integration failure — `PupilEndpointsTests.List_ArmScopedCaller_…`, an unrelated
TASK-0059 test, dying at 12m51s in `SeedSessionAsync` fixture setup with a TLS-level
`SocketException: An existing connection was forcibly closed by the remote host`. Re-run green in
**13 seconds**. The 12m51s-versus-13s gap is the diagnosis: a transient Neon drop, not a defect.
Coverage failed only as a consequence of the unclean run, never on its 60% floor.

**A filtered re-run SILENTLY SKIPPED and still exited 0 — the near-miss of this card.** The first
re-run reported `Failed: 0, Passed: 0, Skipped: 1` with exit code 0, because a raw `dotnet test`
does not resolve `POSTGRES_TEST_CONNECTION`; since TASK-0031 only `ci.ps1` reads it from
`~/.gras/pg-test.txt`. Taking that exit code at face value would have closed the card on a test
that never ran — precisely the false-close `gates.md` §5 calls this project's costliest defect.
**The standing lesson, now demonstrated rather than recited: `ci.ps1` enforces `Skipped > 0` as a
failure, and a hand-rolled filtered `dotnet test` does NOT. Read the counts, never the exit code.**

**`Idempotency-Key` was added unasked and kept.** The card did not ask for it; `backend-dev` reasoned
that a retried correction would otherwise append a second redundant history row for one real-world
event, and required it the way `ApproveAdmission` does for its counter. Confirmed rather than
reverted, and it now carries a route-specific replay test asserting exactly one history row — which
the agent had not written until asked. A second unasked error code, `pupil.registration_number_
not_issued` (409, correcting a pupil who has no number yet), was likewise kept.

**AC 6 is vacuously satisfied and verified as such.** "Published result snapshots are not rewritten"
has nothing to rewrite: no results module exists, only the documented `IResultSetArmLookup` seam.
The agent said so rather than inventing a snapshot or a test, which is what the card asked for.
Drift filed.

**Two findings from the orchestrator's diff review, neither an AC failure, both now drift.** The
handler has no guard for a correction where the new number equals the pupil's current one, and
TASK-0051's issuance path does not check the new history table. Full text in `drift/2026-Q3.md`.

## 2026-09-16 (TASK-0061) — `GET /pupils` class-progression sort, closed on the first scoped gate

`GET /pupils` now returns 6.5.15's order — level, then arm, then surname, then id — with unenrolled
pupils in one flat trailing block per the human's 2026-09-15 ruling. Closes the `GET /pupils`
default-sort drift open since 2026-09-09.

**The dispatch-time grep paid for itself.** `PupilListCursor` was shared by `ListAsync` and
`ListAdmissionsQueueAsync`, and widening it in place — the obvious implementation — would have
silently changed the cursor format the `/admissions` screen has consumed since TASK-0064, three
commits earlier. The orchestrator found this by grepping the tree before dispatch, wrote the two
call sites into the card by line number with an explicit "do not widen in place", and added a
matching acceptance criterion. The agent shipped a separate `PupilRegisterCursor` and left the
queue path byte-for-byte untouched. **Card sufficiency is what prevented this, not review.**

**A real provider limitation, found empirically and worth keeping.** Npgsql/EF Core refuses to
translate `string.Compare`/`string.CompareOrdinal` the moment any other condition — a join, a
correlated subquery, even a `Contains()` or a second `.Where()` — shares the same compiled query,
regardless of shape. The shipped design works around it by splitting the keyset read in two: an
EXISTS-based "strictly after the cursor's bucket" query that is fully server-side and needs no
string comparison, plus a bounded "same bucket as the cursor" query fetched whole and tie-broken in
plain .NET. Every shape tried and rejected is in the card's own `## Log`.

**That workaround is also the source of a latent defect — see `drift/2026-Q3.md`.** The two halves
compare strings differently (SQL collation vs .NET ordinal), which can drop a pupil at a page seam.
It is NOT an acceptance-criterion failure — the card asked for a cross-page-boundary test and got
one — but the test seeds ASCII-only surnames, so it cannot see the case that breaks. Carded as
TASK-0068 rather than reopening this card.

**Gate: the first card closed under the new scoped policy** (`rules/gates.md` §0, same-day human
directive). Build `-warnaserror` clean, format clean, unit **700/700**, architecture **33/33**,
pupils integration slice **34/34**, all `Skipped: 0`; vulnerable dependencies clean; gitleaks clean
on both the history and working-tree passes; `contracts/openapi.json` byte-unchanged. Minutes
instead of the ~1 hour the full run costs.

**Assembling that scoped gate BY HAND went wrong three times in two cards, and none of the three
was a code defect.** (1) On TASK-0063 a filtered `dotnet test` reported `Skipped: 1` and exited 0,
because only `ci.ps1` resolves `POSTGRES_TEST_CONNECTION`. (2) Here, architecture tests failed 13/33
on a staleness guard because the hand-run sequence omitted `ci.ps1`'s gate-4 OpenAPI regeneration.
(3) Then gitleaks exited 1 because the hand-run invocation dropped `--config .gitleaks.toml` and
swept `node_modules`. Each cost a cycle and each looked, briefly, like a real failure. **This is the
evidence for TASK-0067** — until `ci.ps1` takes `-SkipIntegration`/`-IntegrationFilter`, every card
pays an orchestrator re-deriving a nine-gate sequence from memory and getting a piece of it wrong.

## 2026-09-16 (TASK-0067) — `ci.ps1` scoped mode, closed

`-SkipIntegration` and `-IntegrationFilter <expr>`, mutually exclusive, following
`-SkipContractDrift`'s existing parameter and gate-reporting shape. One file touched,
`backend/scripts/ci.ps1`, +163/-16. Contract unmoved; the drift gate passed on every run.

**What the card was really about: keeping two kinds of "skipped" from ever reading alike.**
A stage the orchestrator chose not to run is reported `SKIP: ... (SKIPPED BY REQUEST)` and never
counted as a pass. A test that ran and reported itself skipped — Postgres unreachable, say — still
fails the run in every mode, with `-AllowSkipped` untouched. The coverage gate reports `N/A` rather
than `FAIL` under either flag, because a partial run's coverage number is meaningless but that is
not the same as the floor being breached; without this the flag would be useless, since the run
would still exit non-zero.

**Review found one false-green hole and it was fixed before close.** The first implementation
picked "newest `.trx` by `LastWriteTime`" to detect a zero-match filter. But the Unit &
architecture gate wipes and repopulates `./artifacts/coverage` at `ci.ps1:243`, so that directory
always holds the unit suite's own `.trx` by the time the integration gate runs. Had a filtered run
produced no `.trx` of its own, the check would have selected the UNIT suite's file, read its total
(733, never zero) and passed — the exact false-green class the card exists to kill. Replaced with a
before/after path snapshot: only a `.trx` path absent before this run counts as this run's result.
Comparing file identity rather than timestamps sidesteps clock-resolution questions entirely.
**The hole was narrow — a typo'd filter does still write a `total=0` trx, and a crashed run is
caught by the exit code — but the check read as though it covered a case it did not.**

**Gates.** Default mode: 1045 tests, Skipped 0, 10/10, line 81.97% (pre-hardening file). Matching
filter: 738/738, Skipped 0, coverage N/A, exit 0. Non-matching filter: `FAIL: Integration tests --
matched ZERO tests`, exit 1. All three re-verified by the ORCHESTRATOR against the hardened file,
not accepted on report.

**The default run against the hardened file came back 1039/1045 with 6 failures, all triaged as
network.** Every one a Npgsql `TimeoutException` on connection open, five at exactly the 15s
timeout boundary, all six in `AdmissionApprovalEndpointsTests`, none reaching an assertion.
Re-running that class alone: 21/21, Skipped 0, 5m30s. Skipped was 0 and the total matched the green
run exactly, so this was never the silent-skip trap.

**A fully green default-mode run against the hardened file has NOT been observed.** What exists is
a green run of the pre-hardening file, plus a post-hardening run whose only failures were network
and whose affected slice re-ran green. The default-path delta between the two versions is one
unconditional `Get-ChildItem` into a variable that is never read when `-IntegrationFilter` is
empty. Recorded as accepted reasoning, not as proof. CI on `staging` runs the full suite and is the
proper place that proof lands.

**Process incident: the orchestrator caused a second gate concurrency collision.** It started its
own verification runs while the dev agent was resumed and running its own, putting two `ci.ps1`
processes on one Neon endpoint and one `artifacts/coverage` directory — which the unit gate wipes
mid-flight. Cost one 50-minute run, exit 1 with no diagnosis available. **Compounding it, the
orchestrator captured the run with `>`, which in PowerShell 5.1 takes stdout only, while `ci.ps1`
writes every verdict via `Write-Host` to the information stream — so the log held raw `dotnet`
output and not one `PASS:`/`FAIL:` line.** `6>&1` fixes it. Same root cause as the 2026-09-15
entry: the orchestrator ran a gate against shared state without checking what else was running.

**The card paid for itself inside its own closure.** Diagnosing the six failures needed one class
re-run: 5m30s scoped, against 50m19s for the full integration stage.

## 2026-09-16 (spec) — conflicts 1, 2 and 4 settled; §8.4 restated; Phase 1-3 cards written

### The orchestrator was wrong about what was blocking results

Two closure reports and a delivery estimate named "spec conflicts 1 and 2" as awaiting a human
ruling and gating every results card. They were not. `25-open-conflicts-to-resolve.md` records both
as **RESOLVED by the school**, and `04-module-school-settings.md` §6.2.13 was already written to
those resolutions. The error came from reading the conflicts file's framing — "open conflicts" in
the filename, HIGH markers on the items — rather than its status table.

What was actually blocking was narrower and more concrete. `13-result-computation-rules.md` line 3
designates its own worked example as the regression fixture: *"the figures in 8.4 should be loaded
as test data and the assertions taken directly from the tables."* §8.4 still computed against the
superseded structure and scale, so the engine had a specified algorithm and no valid test oracle.
`index.md` had flagged exactly this and the flag was not acted on.

**The lesson, and it is a cheap one to repeat:** a document titled "open questions" is not evidence
that its questions are open. Read the status table, not the title.

### How §8.4 was restated

Constraint chosen deliberately: **hold every `subject_total` constant** and redistribute only the
CA split. That keeps §8.4.4 (class statistics), §8.4.5 (subject positions, including the 78 tie),
§8.4.6 (arm position) and §8.4.7 (level position across two arms) valid without touching them, and
confines the change to the CA columns, the grades and the remarks. The alternative — rewriting the
marks freely — would have invalidated four tables of carefully constructed edge cases.

| Item | Was | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Structure | 15/15/10/60 with Assignment | 20/20/60 |
| Scale | Six bands | Nine bands per §6.2.13 |
| Adaeze's grades | A, B, C, A | A, B, C+, A+ |
| Adaeze's overall | A, Excellent | **B, Very good** (average 80.00 unchanged) |
| Musa's Maths grade | F, Fail | **E, Not Now** (total 24 unchanged) |
| Cumulative 80.25 | A, Excellent | B, Very good |

Every figure was re-derived by an independent script before the spec was written: scale contiguity
over 0-100 with no gap or overlap, components summing to 100, each CA pair within its 0-20 bound
and summing to the preserved total, each band resolution, all four class averages at half-up 1dp,
and both cumulative methods. All assertions passed.

**One thing the restatement caught that the old fixture hid.** The old CA total column was headed
`/40`. §6.2.13 forbids the literal 40 appearing in a heading, a message, a fixture or in code,
because it is the sum of the non-examination components and not a constant. 20+20 also happens to
be 40, so a careless restatement would have preserved the violation unchanged. The heading now
carries no maximum and §8.4.2 says why.
